 1 09:42:08       In the month before the full-scale invasion of

 2 09:42:14   Ukraine, Gazprom applied the market power it had built

 4 09:42:24   Europe, creating an artificial scarcity of gas.  Gazprom

 5 09:42:30   disputes this, but the evidence that we have submitted

10 09:42:53   discontinuation in October 2021.  This means long-term

 1 09:44:12   government regulations; it was instrumental in setting

 7 09:44:46   sent on 1 April 2022.  This was one day after the

10 09:45:08   threatened that gas supplies would be interrupted unless

                                      21
 1 09:46:26   to sell PPD's euros at the Moscow Stock Exchange using

18 09:51:49       I move now to my third introductory point, the legal

19 09:51:52   point.  Sanctions imposed by the European Union.

20 09:51:57       The purpose of sanctions is to increase the cost of

21 09:52:01   war for Russia.  In broad terms, the sanctions relevant

22 09:52:06   here prohibited transactions related to the management

23 09:52:10   of resources or related to assets of the Central Bank of

24 09:52:15   Russia or of Russian publicly controlled entities, and

25 09:52:22   they prohibited making available directly or indirectly


                                      24
 1 09:52:26   funds or economic resources to sanctioned entities.

 2 09:52:31       In addition, there were prohibitions to circumvent

 3 09:52:35   sanctions and to satisfy claims in connection with

 4 09:52:39   transactions effected by sanctions.

 5 09:52:43       In the recently published opinion of Advocate

 6 09:52:46   General Biondi in the Reibel matter, it is once again

 7 09:52:52   confirmed that sanctions are a fundament of European

 8 09:52:58   public policy.

 9 09:53:00       It was also confirmed that sanctions are built on

10 09:53:03   the principle that they can have significantly negative

11 09:53:08   consequences for certain economic operators, because

12 09:53:13   that is their purpose.

13 09:53:18       The widespread agreement in the authorities is that

14 09:53:24   EU sanctions must be interpreted broadly to ensure their

15 09:53:29   effectiveness.

16 09:53:33       The payment mechanism imposed by Gazprom breached

17 09:53:38   sanctions in several ways which we set out in detail,

18 09:53:40   but already from the overview that I have shown, it is

19 09:53:46   clear that PPD was forced to instruct Gazprombank to

20 09:53:50   sell euros without knowing the buyer, which obviously

21 09:53:57   entails the risk, and in fact the high likelihood, that

22 09:54:01   euros were made available to sanctioned entities,

23 09:54:05   including the CBR.

24 09:54:08       The European Commission confirmed this in commenting

25 09:54:13   on an application of Croatia's High Commercial Court in


                                      25
 1 09:54:17   the bank guarantee case related to this arbitration as

 2 09:54:22   well as the Croatian Government.

 3 09:54:27       The referral was rejected as procedurally

 4 09:54:30   inadmissible, but that does not change the weight of the

 5 09:54:38   Commission's and the Croatian Government's observations.

 6 09:54:46       It is worth mentioning that in our case, regardless

 7 09:54:50   of the decision on all other issues, sanctions prohibit

 8 09:54:55   any award in favour of Gazprom.

 9 09:54:58       As Advocate General Biondi pointed out in Reibel,

10 09:55:03   the no claims provision prohibits an award of any claims

11 09:55:08   if a transaction or its performance has been affected by

12 09:55:18   sanctions.

13 09:55:20       So the claim itself need not be covered by other

16 09:55:34   of other sanctions provisions, and we submit this is

17 09:55:41   dispositive of all of Gazprom's counterclaims.

18 09:55:48       No award can be made in favour of Gazprom.

19 09:56:01       After having highlighted these points, which we

20 09:56:04   submit are of fundamental importance for the decision of

21 09:56:07   the case, I will now give an overview of our legal

23 09:56:17       The legal consequences of the facts of the case are,

24 09:56:22   first, PPD was not obliged to comply with decree 172 or

25 09:56:29   decree 254 due to the application of sanctions, due to


                                      26
 1 09:56:34   article 102 TFEU, PPD did not consent to the variation

 2 09:56:42   of the contract and did not accept to offer -- the offer

 3 09:56:47   to vary the contract.

 4 09:56:50       Even if PPD had consented to the variation, this

 5 09:56:56   consent is invalid because the variation was avoided

 6 09:57:02   with ex tunc effect as it was procured under duress.

 7 09:57:07   Gazprom was not entitled to rely on decree 172 as

 8 09:57:12   a force majeure event.  Gazprom's letter of 1 April

 9 09:57:17   threatening the suspension of deliveries was therefore

10 09:57:20   an unlawful threat.

11 09:57:22       PPD has not waived the right to avoid the purported

12 09:57:27   variation.

13 09:57:29       Of course, the variations, if they were -- if you

14 09:57:34   find they were validly agreed, are also invalid due to

15 09:57:39   the application of sanctions and due to

16 09:57:42   article 102 TFEU.

17 09:57:45       Second, as a consequence of the above, PPD was

18 09:57:50   entitled to pay in euros and refuse payment in roubles.

19 09:57:54       Third, because PPD had not breached the contract,

20 09:58:00   Gazprom was not entitled to suspend performance

21 09:58:02   in November 2022.

22 09:58:06       Fourth, PPD validly avoided the contract by its

23 09:58:10   letter dated 8 March 2023.

24 09:58:16       Gazprom had committed a fundamental breach of

25 09:58:20   contract by conditioning its contractual obligation to


                                      27
 1 09:58:23   deliver gas to the modification of the contract in its

 2 09:58:27   favour, and because it refused to accept payment in

 3 09:58:32   euros and suspended gas supplies.

 4 09:58:38       Fifth, as a consequence of Gazprom's breach of

 5 09:58:45   contract and the avoidance of the contract, PPD is

 6 09:58:48   entitled to recover the incurred losses resulting from

 7 09:58:54   the failure to supply gas to PPD.

 8 09:58:57       Sixth, and independently of the above, Gazprom is

 9 09:59:03   liable for damages caused by its unlawful market

10 09:59:06   manipulation, which constituted a breach of REMIT,

11 09:59:11   article 102 TFEU, a breach of the requirement to observe

12 09:59:17   good faith, and a duty of care and loyalty in

13 09:59:20   contractual relations, as well as a breach of the

14 09:59:24   requirement not to cause damages contrary to good

15 09:59:28   morals.

16 09:59:31       Seventh, PPD has with its claims validly declared

17 09:59:37   set-off against Gazprom's claim to payment of gas

18 09:59:40   delivered in September and October 2022.

19 09:59:46       Eighth and finally, Gazprom is not entitled to the

20 09:59:50   counterclaim because PPD has not committed a fundamental

21 09:59:55   breach of contract.  Because all of Gazprom's

22 10:00:00   counterclaims fail due to sanctions, Gazprom is not

23 10:00:04   entitled to payment for the September and October 2022

24 10:00:11   gas deliveries due to PPD's set-off.  Gazprom is not

25 10:00:16   entitled to unjust enrichment.  Gazprom is not entitled


                                      28
 1 10:00:20   to the MAQ counterclaim and Gazprom is not entitled to

 2 10:00:27   lost volume seller damage.

 3 10:00:38       I will now move to PPD's claim for suspended gas

 4 10:00:42   deliveries.

 5 10:00:46       First, contrary to Gazprom's submissions, the

 6 10:00:52   amendments of the contract requested by Gazprom required

 7 10:00:57   PPD's consent.

 8 10:01:02       The contract stipulated payment in euros.  When

 9 10:01:06   Gazprom demanded payment in roubles under the payment

10 10:01:11   mechanism, PPD was not obliged to comply with this by

11 10:01:18   virtue of article 54 CISG.  Article 54 provides:

12 10:01:27       "The buyer's obligation to pay the price includes

13 10:01:29   taking such steps and complying with such formalities as

14 10:01:34   may be required under the contract or any laws and

15 10:01:39   regulations to enable payment to be made."

16 10:01:41       There is widespread agreement in legal writing that

17 10:01:48   the CISG does not provide the seller with the right to

18 10:01:53   elect a replacement currency.

19 10:01:57       A right of the seller to choose the payment currency

20 10:02:00   was expressly rejected when drafting the CISG, as the

21 10:02:06   drafting history shows.

22 10:02:09       As Prof Perner notes, there may be a right to elect

23 10:02:17   a replacement currency where it has become impossible to

24 10:02:23   the buyer to pay in the agreed currency.  The reason for

25 10:02:28   this is that the buyer should not be able to rely on


                                      29
 1 10:02:34   foreign exchange controls of his own country to refuse

 2 10:02:39   payment, but that is not the case here.

 3 10:02:42       Even Prof Klicka admits that if a right of the

 4 10:02:48   seller to elect a replacement currency is accepted, the

 5 10:02:52   chosen replacement currency must be suitable and must

 6 10:02:55   not burden the buyer inappropriately.

 7 10:02:59       Therefore, the obligation of the buyer to comply

 8 10:03:03   with steps and formalities to effect payment does not

 9 10:03:07   grant the seller a right to choose a replacement

10 10:03:11   currency.

11 10:03:12       If such right is accepted, then the replacement

12 10:03:15   currency must not burden the buyer inappropriately.

13 10:03:20       However, as the record shows, the payment mechanism

14 10:03:26   introduced a new currency into the contract, imposed

15 10:03:31   significant costs and further very substantial risks on

16 10:03:38   PPD.

17 10:03:38       This therefore does not fall under steps and

18 10:03:43   formalities according to article 54 CISG, and PPD did

19 10:03:49   not have to comply with the payment mechanism.

20 10:03:54       Gazprom not only imposed a change of currency, but

21 10:03:58   also a complex and intransparent payment mechanism

22 10:04:03   involving considerable costs and risks.

23 10:04:08       The decrees imposed financial costs on PPD as

24 10:04:14   payment had to be made three to eight days earlier than

25 10:04:17   under article 9.1 of the contract.


                                      30
 1 10:04:20       This caused significant additional costs as Mr Way

 2 10:04:26   confirmed in his report.

 3 10:04:28       The mechanism imposed by Gazprom was subject to

 4 10:04:34   payment delays due to compliance investigations caused

 5 10:04:40   by the numerous interposed banks and entities, which in

 6 10:04:45   turn resulted in late payments and cut-offs by Gazprom.

 7 10:04:50       The decrees further imposed burdensome processes

 8 10:04:54   without any electronic access to PPD's Gazprombank

 9 10:04:58   account.  PPD was in the dark about what was happening

10 10:05:01   with its payments which, as you know, were very

11 10:05:05   significant sums.

12 10:05:08       The imposed processes were burdensome, as the

13 10:05:13   correspondence on the record shows.

14 10:05:17       Particularly relevant for sanctions, the payment

15 10:05:25   mechanism meant PPD did not have control over the

16 10:05:30   identity of the buyer of euros.  This in itself

17 10:05:35   precludes reliance on article 54 CISG because there

18 10:05:39   cannot be an implied duty to expose oneself to

19 10:05:44   a sanctions risk.

20 10:05:46       And finally, the decrees exposed PPD to the Russian

21 10:05:51   legal system for dispute resolution, adding additional

22 10:05:55   risks to PPD's position.

23 10:05:57       The sums at issue were very significant and it was

24 10:06:01   therefore certainly not a formality which PPD could have

25 10:06:08   been asked to comply under the CISG.


                                      31
 1 10:06:15       The decrees vested the Russian administration with

 2 10:06:20   significant authority to decide on the fulfilment of

 3 10:06:24   PPD's payment obligations under the payment mechanism

 4 10:06:28   which interfered with the contract and which again is

 5 10:06:32   not a formality.

 6 10:06:34       Therefore, the contention that PPD was obliged to

 7 10:06:38   accept the imposed payment mechanism pursuant to

 8 10:06:40   article 54 CISG fails.

 9 10:06:48       Even Prof Klicka seems to recognise this when he

10 10:06:53   suggests that PPD's non-acceptance of the payment

11 10:07:02   mechanism would be "an action against good faith, which

12 10:07:07   results in rendering the respective rejection of the

13 10:07:11   request to amend unlawful under the Contract".

14 10:07:14       This contention evidently has no legal basis,

15 10:07:18   because it is contrary to the fundamental principles of

16 10:07:25   party autonomy and freedom of contract.

17 10:07:30       There is simply no basis for a rule that the party

18 10:07:34   must comply with contract amendments requested by the

19 10:07:37   other party pursuant to unspecified, I quote again,

20 10:07:43   "values incorporated in the contractual provisions [and]

21 10:07:47   various provisions of the CISG".

22 10:07:49       The CISG exhaustively sets out the requirements for

23 10:07:54   valid contract modification.  If article 54 is

24 10:07:58   inapplicable, any modification of the contract requires

25 10:08:03   the agreement of the parties.


                                      32
 1 10:08:05       The inescapable conclusion is that PPD's consent to

 2 10:08:09   the payment mechanism was required under the CISG, which

 3 10:08:13   was not validly given.

 4 10:08:20       Gazprom alternatively tries to argue that PPD only

 5 10:08:24   needed to consent to material changes to the contract in

 6 10:08:28   accordance with article 19 to CISG by analogy.

 7 10:08:35       However, article 29 CISG states in abundantly clear

 8 10:08:44   terms that any contract modification requires the

 9 10:08:47   agreement of the parties.

10 10:08:51       There is no doubt in the case law and in the legal

11 10:08:53   commentaries that after the conclusion of a contract,

12 10:08:59   modifications are governed by article 29 CISG alone and

13 10:09:04   not by article 19.

14 10:09:06       Therefore, agreement to any modification was needed.

15 10:09:12       I move now to the application of these principles to

16 10:09:15   the facts.

17 10:09:18       PPD had signed the 1 April letter, but there was no

18 10:09:23   agreement to allow Gazprom to change the payment

19 10:09:26   mechanism unilaterally at its discretion in the future.

20 10:09:31       However, this is what happened.

21 10:09:36       The CBR's official clarification of 29 April 2022

22 10:09:43   sought to further amend the risk allocation set out in

23 10:09:47   the 1 April letter in case the bank failed to make the

24 10:09:52   payment.

25 10:09:56       Decree 254 introduced the NCC, another party, to the


                                      33
 1 10:10:00   payment mechanism, which was evidently not part of, part

 2 10:10:08   and parcel of the initial offer to modify the contract,

 3 10:10:10   as Gazprom submits.

 4 10:10:15       Gazprom's own witnesses confirm that Gazprom did not

 5 10:10:19   seek consent to apply decree 254 and PPD did not consent

 6 10:10:25   to any further modifications.

 7 10:10:29       Gazprom simply notified PPD of changes, which can

 8 10:10:34   have no contractual effect.

 9 10:10:37       There were no offers, and therefore nothing could be

10 10:10:42   accepted.

11 10:10:44       Therefore, in conclusion, PPD never consented to the

12 10:10:48   modified payment mechanism set out in Gazprom's letter

13 10:10:51   of 5 May 2022.

14 10:10:58       Gazprom alternatively says that PPD impliedly

15 10:11:05   consented to the change of the payment mechanism by its

16 10:11:09   conduct in accordance with article 18 CISG.  This fails

17 10:11:15   because silence or continued performance of the contract

18 10:11:20   cannot in the absence of a of clear indications to the

19 10:11:25   contrary, be interpreted as consent to modification of

20 10:11:29   the contract.

21 10:11:30       This applies with particular force after the start

22 10:11:34   of the performance of the contract as Prof Schroeter

23 10:11:39   confirms.  There are no such clear indications of

24 10:11:44   consent on the record, rather there are clear statements

25 10:11:48   of disagreement.


                                      34
 1 10:11:52       As a last resort Gazprom says that it relied on

 2 10:11:57   PPD's conduct and that this reliance would be protected.

 3 10:12:01   However, under article 29(2) of the CISG, the protection

 4 10:12:08   of reliance is limited and ends when a contrary

 5 10:12:13   information is given.

 6 10:12:15       The fact show that PPD never gave rise to legitimate

 7 10:12:23   trust on Gazprom's side.  With letter 26 May 2022, PPD

 8 10:12:29   expressly stated that it did not agree to any

 9 10:12:33   contractual amendment.  PPD never made any payment under

10 10:12:39   the payment mechanism set out by decree 172.

11 10:12:46       Equally, no payments were made under the payment

12 10:12:50   mechanism before PPD's letter of 26 May 2022, in which

13 10:12:57   PPD made clear that it did not agree to any amendment of

14 10:13:02   the contract.

15 10:13:04       Any reliance of Gazprom under article 29(2) had been

16 10:13:09   expressly destroyed by PPD with its letter of

17 10:13:13   26 May 2022, even if one assumes there were such

18 10:13:20   a reliance.

19 10:13:21       In conclusion, PPD has not validly consented to the

20 10:13:26   amendment payment mechanism and Gazprom also cannot

21 10:13:30   invoke protection of reliance.

22 10:13:42       I move to the next point, duress.

23 10:13:49       Even if it is found that PPD had consented to the

24 10:13:52   payment mechanism of the 1 April letter, and no consent

25 10:13:59   was needed for any subsequent change to the payment


                                      35
 1 10:14:02   mechanism, this consent is invalid because it was given

 2 10:14:08   under duress.

 3 10:14:11       In accordance with section 870 of the Austrian Civil

 4 10:14:15   Code, if a threatened party was not free to form its

 5 10:14:20   will out of fear that the threatened harm would occur,

 6 10:14:26   the threatened party can avoid the contract with

 7 10:14:30   retroactive effect.

 8 10:14:31       Section 870 protects the free will of the party who

 9 10:14:37   was forced to agree to a contract under duress.

10 10:14:43       The legal prerequisites to avoid a contract in

11 10:14:46   accordance with section 870 are, first, an

12 10:14:51   unjust/unlawful threat; second, well-founded fear, and

13 10:15:01   third, a causal link between the threat and the

14 10:15:06   agreement to the contract or the contract amendment.

15 10:15:10       I will now discuss these elements.

16 10:15:14       In its 1 April letter, Gazprom clearly made a threat

17 10:15:19   because it announced PPD would not receive gas under the

18 10:15:23   contract unless it consented to the amended payment

19 10:15:27   mechanism.

20 10:15:28       As Prof Perner notes, this was not an amicable

21 10:15:32   communication, this was a written ultimatum.

22 10:15:38       In legal terms, a threat is the announcement of

23 10:15:42   a severely negative consequence should the counterparty

24 10:15:51   not act accordingly that is agreed to the contract or

25 10:15:55   the contract amendment.


                                      36
 1 10:15:57       A threat does not have to be made explicitly, it can

 2 10:16:00   be hidden or implied.

 3 10:16:04       Here we have an explicit threat.  Gazprom explicitly

 4 10:16:11   announced a severely negative consequence, the cessation

 5 10:16:14   of gas supplies, on which PPD heavily depended, should

 6 10:16:20   PPD not agree to the amendment of the contract.

 7 10:16:25       The intention of the threatening party to make

 8 10:16:30   a threat is not a prerequisite for the avoidance of the

 9 10:16:36   contract.  Section 870 does not even require the

10 10:16:40   threatened party's awareness of wrongdoing, contrary to

11 10:16:44   what Gazprom wrongly alleges.

12 10:16:49       Gazprom in essence says: we just informed you about

13 10:16:55   governmental regulations in Russia that are binding on

14 10:16:58   us.

15 10:17:00       However, as you have seen already from the facts

16 10:17:04   summarised in the introduction, Gazprom is not an

17 10:17:07   ordinary corporation that was simply subject to

18 10:17:10   government regulation.  It was complicit with the

19 10:17:14   Russian State and collaborated with it precisely with

20 10:17:20   the purpose of interfering with gas contracts.

21 10:17:24       As Mr Miller said, "Our product, our rules", and

22 10:17:28   with "our", he clearly means Gazprom and Russia.

23 10:17:33       The 1 April letter was therefore not only a threat,

24 10:17:38   but it was an unlawful threat that was not justified by

25 10:17:43   bona fide government regulations.


                                      37
 1 10:17:49       There is a general principle that state entities

 2 10:17:51   cannot rely on the separate legal entity from the state

 3 10:17:55   to the detriment of its contract partners, which was

 4 10:18:00   formulated by Prof Bockstiegel and which is therefore

 5 10:18:04   called the Bockstiegel test.  According to this test

 6 10:18:10   there is the presumption that a state will not have its

 7 10:18:14   executive organs act to the detriment of its own

 8 10:18:18   institutions, including state enterprises.  Therefore,

 9 10:18:22   administrative acts of state are in principle not

10 10:18:26   considered force majeure and therefore in the context of

11 10:18:31   duress they are not considered as an excuse for making

12 10:18:39   a threat.

13 10:18:42       This presumption is not applied if it can be seen

14 10:18:46   prima facie or can be proved by the state enterprise

15 10:18:50   that the administrative act was caused by general

16 10:18:53   considerations not connected with this contract or this

17 10:18:58   sort of contract.

18 10:19:00       The primary presumption is applicable again if the

19 10:19:03   private party proves that in its specific case, general

20 10:19:09   considerations did not apply.

21 10:19:21       Decree 172 was not the general law, but an

22 10:19:26   administrative act of state that specifically targeted

23 10:19:32   a particular class of contracts.

24 10:19:35       It specifically aimed to interfere with the

25 10:19:38   contractual relationships of Gazprom with its contract


                                      38
 1 10:19:42   partners in unfriendly countries.

 2 10:19:46       It applied to Gazprom alone, which had the expert

 3 10:19:50   monopoly, and targeted a specific subsection of

 4 10:19:54   Gazprom's contract portfolio.

 5 10:19:58       Decree 172 aimed to amend the gas supply contracts

 6 10:20:05   to the detriment of Gazprom's contract partners, and to

 7 10:20:08   benefit the strategic interest of the Russian State and

 8 10:20:13   of Gazprom.

 9 10:20:20       The amendments of the contracts were to be achieved

10 10:20:22   without negotiation, without any concession on Gazprom's

11 10:20:27   part, as also the documents produced by Gazprom show.

12 10:20:35       Following the basic presumption, as an

13 10:20:38   administrative act of state cannot be considered force

14 10:20:44   majeure, Gazprom has the burden to prove the contrary,

15 10:20:49   to show that decree 172 was caused by general

16 10:20:56   considerations not connected with the contract or this

17 10:20:59   sort of contract.

18 10:21:00       But Gazprom cannot prove this.  As Gazprom's public

19 10:21:05   statements and the record shows, Gazprom was not opposed

20 10:21:11   to the interference with contracts, but it was complicit

21 10:21:16   with it.

22 10:21:18       Therefore, government regulation cannot justify the

23 10:21:24   threat made by Gazprom.

24 10:21:30       There is clear authority in Austrian law that the

25 10:21:33   threat of a breach of contract amounts to unlawful means


                                      39
 1 10:21:38   and is therefore unlawful.

 2 10:21:45       With its letter dated 1 April, Gazprom conditioned

 3 10:21:51   the performance of its delivery obligations on the

 4 10:21:54   acceptance of amended payment terms.  This was, in line

 5 10:22:01   with the jurisprudence and the legal commentaries, an

 6 10:22:05   unlawful threat in the sense of section 870.

 7 10:22:13       The second element of duress is well-founded fear.

 8 10:22:21   It is recognised that a threat with breach of contract

 9 10:22:27   triggers reasonable fear if the threatened party is

10 10:22:30   urgently dependent on the performance of an existing

11 10:22:34   contract.

12 10:22:36       On the facts, there can be no doubt that PPD was

13 10:22:41   urgently dependent on Gazprom's gas and that its fear of

14 10:22:46   the consequences of not signing the letter were real and

15 10:22:50   well founded.

16 10:22:52       As the testimony of Mr Vujnovac and Ms Measic shows,

17 10:22:57   they reasonably feared that the end of gas supplies from

18 10:23:02   Gazprom would have catastrophic consequences.

19 10:23:07       Finally, section 870 requires a causal link between

20 10:23:12   the threat and the conclusion of the contract amendment.

21 10:23:17       Only the perspective of the threatened party is

22 10:23:20   relevant, it must be examined whether the threatened

23 10:23:23   party would also have concluded the contract amendment

24 10:23:28   in this specific form without the threat.  Contributory

25 10:23:34   causation is sufficient.


                                      40
 1 10:23:39       On the facts it is clear that Gazprom's threat was

 2 10:23:43   the cause of the conclusion of the contract amendment in

 3 10:23:48   the sense of section 870.

 4 10:23:51       Had it not been for Gazprom's threat, PPD would not

 5 10:23:54   have countersigned Gazprom 1 April letter in this

 6 10:23:59   specific form on 4 April 2022.

 7 10:24:04       This was -- the threat was the only cause.  PPD had

 8 10:24:11   no reason whatsoever to conclude a contract amendment

 9 10:24:14   that brought no benefits but only disadvantages.

10 10:24:19       The conclusion is that the countersignature of the

11 10:24:23   1 April letter was made under duress and was therefore

12 10:24:29   validly avoided.

13 10:24:32       Gazprom says that if there was duress, PPD waived

14 10:24:38   its rights to challenge the purported variation through

15 10:24:41   its subsequent conduct.

16 10:24:46       The applicable standard for an implied waiver is

17 10:24:50   particularly strict under Austrian law.  A waiver can

18 10:24:55   only be assumed if there is conduct that leaves no

19 10:24:59   reasonable doubt with respect to the intention to waive

20 10:25:04   a right, which will rarely be given.

21 10:25:09       The case law of the Supreme Court confirms that

22 10:25:13   extreme caution is required when it comes to implied

23 10:25:18   declarations of intent in the context of waivers.

24 10:25:24       In addition, as Prof Perner stresses, there can be

25 10:25:33   no waiver as long as the situation of duress continues.


                                      41
 1 10:25:39       PPD's fears could at the earliest have ended when it

 2 10:25:44   was possible for it to receive replacement supplies of

 3 10:25:48   gas and this was available only in November 2022, when

 4 10:25:53   PPD clearly expressed not to be bound by the purported

 5 10:25:58   contract amendment.

 6 10:26:00       There is therefore no basis for Gazprom's assertion

 7 10:26:04   that PPD had waived its right to challenge the purported

 8 10:26:08   variation.

 9 10:26:18       I will now deal with Gazprom's reliance on force

10 10:26:21   majeure.

11 10:26:25       Under article 6 CISG, the parties are free to

12 10:26:31   supplement, replace or even discard provisions of the

13 10:26:38   CISG.  Therefore, the parties were able to fully or

14 10:26:45   partially derogate from article 79 CISG.  Accordingly,

15 10:26:52   with article 11 of the contract, the parties derogated

16 10:26:56   from the CISG and agreed on a detailed code to

17 10:27:02   extensively regulate force majeure events, including the

18 10:27:05   steps required to invoke a force majeure event, which

19 10:27:12   replaced the regime of the CISG.

20 10:27:16       Under article 11.4 and 11.5 of the contract, Gazprom

21 10:27:21   was obliged to immediately notify PPD upon the

22 10:27:24   occurrence of a force majeure event and to provide

23 10:27:28   a confirmation of the occurrence and the duration of the

24 10:27:32   force majeure event by the chamber of commence and

25 10:27:35   industry of the Russian Federation.


                                      42
 1 10:27:39       As Prof Perner confirms, with this, the parties have

 2 10:27:43   agreed upon formalities which must be complied with and

 3 10:27:49   they have validly derogated from the regime of the CISG.

 4 10:27:55       Gazprom claims that article 79 CISG should

 5 10:28:01   nevertheless apply as a default rule.  However, as

 6 10:28:06   Prof Perner notes, this submission is impossible; it is

 7 10:28:13   impossible to conceive a reason why the terms of the

 8 10:28:18   contract should simply be disregarded, because that's

 9 10:28:24   what Gazprom's position amounts to.

10 10:28:30       On the facts, there is no doubt that Gazprom did not

11 10:28:33   comply with the procedures set out in article 11 of the

12 10:28:36   contract, Gazprom did not make a force majeure

13 10:28:44   notification.

14 10:28:45       The letters of 1 April, 31 October and

15 10:28:47   3 November 2022 were not force majeure notifications.

16 10:28:57   Also, Gazprom's own witnesses did not understand

17 10:29:01   Gazprom's 1 April letter to be a force majeure notice as

18 10:29:07   required by the contract.

19 10:29:11       A certificate of the Chamber of Commerce & Industry

20 10:29:14   of the Russian Federation dated 2 December 2024 was only

21 10:29:19   submitted two and a half years later in the course of

22 10:29:24   this arbitration which is evidently not timely.

23 10:29:29       The consequence of Gazprom's non-compliance with the

24 10:29:32   prerequisites of article 11 of the contract is that the

25 10:29:36   invocation of force majeure must fail.


                                      43
 1 10:29:40       In any event, as I have outlined before and I'm not

 2 10:29:45   going to repeat it here, there was no force majeure

 3 10:29:50   event because Gazprom has promoted the government

 4 10:29:54   regulation it now claims was force majeure.

 5 10:30:00       The contractual definition of force majeure is not

 6 10:30:06   met, and also under the Bockstiegel test the reliance on

 7 10:30:11   force majeure fails.

 8 10:30:14       In addition to this, the party affected by force

 9 10:30:17   majeure must do all in its powers to avoid the effect of

10 10:30:22   the force majeure event and to ensure contractual

11 10:30:26   performance, as the contract confirms.  Gazprom did not

12 10:30:32   do this.

13 10:30:34       There was a possibility to seek an exemption from

14 10:30:38   the effects of decree 172.  Gazprom could at least have

15 10:30:44   filed application with Russian Government bodies to

16 10:30:47   obtain an exemption.  Gazprom could also have delivered

17 10:30:52   gas via its subsidiaries in the EU.  Gazprom did not do

18 10:30:59   this.

19 10:31:01       Therefore, Gazprom did not comply with its duty to

20 10:31:05   do all in its power to carry out its obligations

21 10:31:08   required under the contract, and the reliance on force

22 10:31:13   majeure also fails for this reason.

 7 10:45:01   of a contract.

 1 12:39:53       Second, it follows that gas that was replaced is

 2 13:18:44   an anticipatory breach.  Prof Perner explains:

 4 13:18:52   under a contract, the counterparty can never invoke

 5 13:18:56   Art 71 CISG and suspend its own performance."

 6 13:19:01       Article 80 further supports PPD's position.  It

 7 13:19:06   provides:

 8 13:19:07       "a party may not rely on a failure of the other

 9 13:19:10   party to perform, to the extent that such failure was

10 13:19:14   caused by the first party's [breach] or omission."

11 13:19:17       Gazprom asserts that even if PPD's non-payment via

12 13:19:23   the mechanism is justified by circumstances beyond the

13 13:19:27   party's control, Gazprom still had a right to suspend

14 13:19:31   performance.

15 13:19:32       This argument must be rejected.  PPD was not

16 13:19:36   affected by any impediment to performance within the

17 13:19:40   meaning of article 71.  PPD always had the ability to

18 13:19:44   pay in euros.

19 13:19:46       The very same author upon which Gazprom relies

20 13:19:49   clarifies that article 80 here comes into play.

21 13:19:54       Article 80 must also be taken into account when

22 13:19:58   assessing the breach of duty.  Suspension is not

23 13:20:01   permissible if the creditor is responsible for the

24 13:20:05   debtor's breach.

25 13:20:07       Issuance of decree 992 does not change this


                                     119
 1 13:20:11   conclusion.  What decree 992 did, it allowed payments of

 2 13:20:21   debt by foreign buyer in euros.  However, it continued

 1 13:33:37       What is Gazprom asking?  Gazprom says PPD owes

 9 13:34:12       The section 1041 on which Gazprom relies is

10 13:34:17   applicable, it is a subsidiary norm which is applicable

11 13:34:21   where there is no contract.  In the case of the absence

12 13:34:24   of contract, it is based on a basic idea of reverse

13 13:34:30   unjustified transfers of assets.

14 13:34:33       Of course, what was transferred here?  It was gas.

15 13:34:37   Was that transfer unjustified?  Not, because the

16 13:34:41   contract is a basis for that transfer.

17 13:34:44       The legal prerequisites are simply not met.

18 13:34:48       This section does not apply where the contract

19 13:34:51   exists.  This is that elementary.  Unjust enrichment law

20 13:34:56   is designed to reverse unjustified transfers in

21 13:35:03   situations precisely where there is no contractual basis

22 13:35:06   for the benefit received.

21 15:39:37   balance, they are not individually identifiable.  They

22 15:39:39   are not earmarked or segregated by reference to the

23 15:39:42   party on whose behalf a particular exchange transaction

24 15:39:45   may be executed.

25 15:39:46       Clearly, as in any exchange transaction of this


                                     169
 1 15:39:49   type, PPD cannot claim any proprietary rights over this

 2 15:39:54   GPB euro account with NCC.

 3 15:39:59       Gazprombank, if it has the collateral, as the next

 4 15:40:02   step may enter organised trading.

 5 15:40:06       It enters into sale and purchase transactions with

 6 15:40:10   NCC.  NCC is a clearing house.  As at any exchange.

 7 15:40:16   Everyone makes transactions necessarily and solely with

 8 15:40:20   NCC.  There is no way to go around it in case of MOEX.

 9 15:40:26       It's a mandatory party to any transaction and, as at

10 15:40:30   any organised trades, no one can make transactions

11 15:40:34   directly excluding NCC.

12 15:40:38       It is crucial to understand that in forex trade

13 15:40:42   Gazprombank does not actually exchange euros to roubles,

14 15:40:45   like we do when we go to the exchange office at the

15 15:40:51   Taksim Square.

16 15:40:53       At forex trade there are two different and

17 15:40:56   independent transactions.  GPB sells euro to NCC and

18 15:41:00   then, separately, GPB buys roubles from NCC.  And

19 15:41:05   effectively, when I change euro to liras in the Taksim

20 15:41:08   Square, I exchange one asset for the other, but in the

21 15:41:11   organised trades it is not how it works.  It's not what

22 15:41:15   happens.

23 15:41:16       GPB separately sells 100 euro to NCC and then,

24 15:41:20   separately -- and then actually what happens is that NCC

25 15:41:25   records on the account of GPB that euro has been sold.


                                     170
 1 15:41:30   And then, separately, GPB purchases say 5,000 roubles

 2 15:41:35   from NCC, and NCC reflects this on the GPB rouble

 3 15:41:39   account, say plus 5,000 roubles.

 4 15:41:43       At the end of the day, what happens is called

 5 15:41:45   netting the settlement.  And at that moment in time, all

 6 15:41:49   claims of all bidders through the day are settled

 7 15:41:53   between NCC and all of the bidders.

 8 15:41:56       That is the essence of the clearing mechanism.  So

 9 15:41:58   if you enter into a transaction directly with another

10 15:42:01   bidder, you face the risk that a transaction may fail

11 15:42:05   because they or the party may fail.  However, if your

12 15:42:09   counterparty is a clearing house, that risk is excluded.

13 15:42:12       A clearing house mitigates the risks by the

14 15:42:16   mandatory requirement to pre-post collateral at the full

15 15:42:20   amount of the transaction, so if the bidder does not

16 15:42:23   post collateral covering its future obligations under

17 15:42:25   the transaction, it simply cannot be admitted to

18 15:42:28   participate in the process.

19 15:42:32       Hence, when PPD states that clearing through NCC

20 15:42:36   posted additional risk that the clearing mechanism may

21 15:42:39   potentially fail and, plainly speaking, NCC would be out

22 15:42:43   of money to perform its obligations -- that is one of

23 15:42:46   the arguments in the submissions -- then, well, it is to

24 15:42:49   be, to say mildly, naive, and demonstrates the lack of

25 15:42:53   understanding how it works, because there is necessarily


                                     171
 1 15:42:55   a collateral.  Without a collateral of GPB funds, own

 2 15:43:01   funds or some other funds they may have in a big pool,

 3 15:43:04   there can be no trade.

 4 15:43:07       Once GPB has the funds back on its account at NCC

 5 15:43:11   after netting, it is now free to withdraw them and

 6 15:43:15   dispose of them as it wishes.

 7 15:43:17       So now, upon instruction of PPD, GPB would

 8 15:43:19   accordingly credit the funds in roubles to the GPE's

20 16:11:13       Article 54 CISG provides that a buyer is obliged to

21 16:11:16   take such steps and comply with such formalities as may

22 16:11:20   be required under any laws and regulations to make the

23 16:11:24   payment.  This was cited today by the other party, with

24 16:11:28   no emphasis put towards any laws and regulations.  But

25 16:11:32   these are the key words.


                                     190
 1 16:11:34       The purpose of this is to clarify that the buyer's

 2 16:11:37   obligation to pay is not limited to mechanical transfer

 3 16:11:41   of money from one account to another.  That may also

 4 16:11:44   require other action to ensure that payment is effected.

 5 16:11:49   The latter, effective performance of the payment

 6 16:11:50   obligation, is the core of this provision, article 54.

 7 16:11:55       On this basis, the applicable steps and formalities

 8 16:11:58   are defined as the moment of performance, and not the

 9 16:12:01   moment of conclusion of the contract.  And it's the

10 16:12:04   buyer's burden to apply due diligence and ensure that

11 16:12:07   the payment was effected.

12 16:12:09       The word "any" was also used on purpose to indicate

13 16:12:12   that the laws and regulations of either buyer or the

14 16:12:15   seller's place of business are applicable.  Nor does

15 16:12:19   article 54 limit the scope of relevant regulations to

16 16:12:22   applicable laws.

17 16:12:23       The actual test is rather functional here: whether

18 16:12:26   the regulation affects the mode of performance of the

19 16:12:28   payment obligation or not.

20 16:12:31       Decree 172 met all the prongs of article 54.  It was

21 16:12:37   envisaged by regulation, did not require claimant to pay

22 16:12:40   in a different currency, or pay a different sum, or do

23 16:12:44   anything beyond the scope of its obligation to pay.  It

24 16:12:47   merely required to initiate payment two business days

25 16:23:00       To put it simply, article 29.2 of the CISG was


                                     198
 1 16:23:04   designed to protect trust induced by the other party's

13 16:26:43       The claimant's contention that respondent imposed

14 16:26:45   the modification on claimant via threat and duress is

15 16:26:51   plainly wrong.

16 16:26:53       The letter of 1 April 2022 was nothing more than

17 16:26:58   a notification of new requirements that are contained in

18 16:27:01   a binding decree, making it thus impossible for the

19 16:27:05   respondent to continue supplies under the contract

20 16:27:08   unless the decree was complied with and the contract was

21 16:27:14   accordingly altered.

22 16:27:15       If a person suffers from the pressure of

23 16:27:18   circumstances, without this pressure being wrongfully

24 16:27:21   exercised by the other party, this is not enough to

25 16:27:23   establish duress.


                                     201
 1 16:27:26       As the Supreme Court of Austria phrases it:

 2 16:27:29       "every pressure creates the pressure of

 3 16:27:30   circumstances, however, not every person exposed to the

 4 16:27:33   pressure of circumstances is in such a position due to

 5 16:27:36   a wrongfully exercise pressure."

 6 16:27:39       There was no wrongfully exercised pressure by GPE in

 7 16:27:42   this case.

 8 16:27:44       The threefold test that is presented by PPD for the

 9 16:27:48   duress finds absolutely no grounds in the facts of the

10 16:27:52   dispute with the opposite not proven by the claimant.

11 16:27:57       The action can neither be a lawful or an unlawful

12 16:28:00   threat if it is not a threat at all.

13 16:28:03       And there can be no causal link between a threat and

14 16:28:09   a party's declaration of intent to conclude a contract

15 16:28:11   if there is no threat.

16 16:28:15       The present case is a matching example of the

17 16:28:19   Supreme Court finding.  The only and one reason for

18 16:28:21   respondent to suggest the amendment of the contract, as

19 16:28:24   well as to all other respondent's European buyers as

20 16:28:27   well on 1 April 2022, was the enactment of the decree,

21 16:28:32   having its unavoidable effect on both parties in spite

22 16:28:35   of, notwithstanding, the GPE's will.

23 16:28:39       It is like the atmospheric pressure: I can like it

24 16:28:42   or dislike it, but it exists.

25 16:28:44       The decree applies irrespective of what the


                                     202
 1 16:28:47   respondent wishes or thinks.  The decree authorised the

 2 16:28:49   central energy customs to monitor compliance with the

 3 16:28:52   decree and, if violated, prohibit supplies.  Not GPE,

 4 16:28:57   but the central energy customs has control over the

 5 16:29:00   infrastructure.

 6 16:29:02       Mr Perner, expert of the claimant, fails to take

 7 16:29:05   into account the above precedent of the Austrian Supreme

 8 16:29:08   Court, which actually distinguishes the pressure of

 9 16:29:11   circumstances from the pressure of the unlawful threat,

10 16:29:13   and that results in a logical fallacy.

11 16:29:17       Following his logic, even a letter about an

12 16:29:20   inadvertently falling meteorite, in the hypothetical

 1 17:30:44   of CISG, the impediment, force majeure, is an event or

 4 17:30:56   reasonably foreseeable at the moment of the conclusion

 1 17:33:45       It lies with the claimant.  Contrary to the

 3 17:33:50   separate legal personality of an entity is the

 4 17:33:54   presumption and if you want to demonstrate the contrary,

 5 17:33:59   a lot of evidence has to be provided and assessed so

 6 17:34:04   that to be sufficient.

 7 17:34:08       On the question of whether the general presumption

 8 17:34:10   of separate legal personality can be rebutted, in which

 9 17:34:13   cases both parties relied on the so-called Bockstiegel

10 17:34:19   test.  That was already mentioned today.  Well, it

11 17:34:21   essentially represents a number of reasoned conclusions

12 17:34:24   of Prof Bockstiegel, based on analysis of cases where

13 17:34:30   those issues arose.

14 17:34:32       This set of conclusions was adopted by many

15 17:34:34   tribunals as an authority guidance.  Clearly the

16 17:34:37   tribunal is not bound by this test, but to begin with,

17 17:34:41   we shall clarify that what claimant presents as two

18 17:34:46   tests, general law and administrative law, is in fact

19 17:34:51   a rule for the allocation of the burden of proof, rather

20 17:34:54   than a substantive criterion allowing to define whether

21 17:34:57   it was a general law or administrative law.  That we'll

22 17:35:01   look at.

23 17:35:02       Under rule A of this Bockstiegel test, if an act

24 17:35:06   qualifies as administrative act, the presumption is that

25 17:35:10   it does not constitute force majeure for a state


                                     231
 1 17:35:13   enterprise, and the latter therefore bears the burden of

 2 17:35:18   proving the opposite.

 3 17:35:20       So if it's an administrative act, the presumption is

 4 17:35:24   that the act which is administrative does not constitute

 5 17:35:28   force majeure, and the enterprise has to rebut the

 6 17:35:32   charge.

 7 17:35:33       In particular, the presumption may be rebutted by

 8 17:35:36   the fact that the administrative act was based on

 9 17:35:39   general considerations.

10 17:35:43       Under rule B, if an act qualifies as a general law,

11 17:35:47   the presumption is that a state enterprise may invoke

12 17:35:51   force majeure and the other party shall disprove it.

13 17:35:55   However, what constitutes a general law and general

14 17:35:58   considerations is a different question.

15 17:36:01       The answer to this question lies with the purpose of

16 17:36:03   the public act and the interests pursued by the state in

17 17:36:06   its adoption.

18 17:36:08       So essentially, the tribunal needs to establish an

19 17:36:11   abuse of public authority for commercial benefit of the

20 17:36:15   state as a shareholder of a state company, that the

21 17:36:19   state abused its public authority to meet its commercial

22 17:36:22   interests, to find that the state enterprise is barred

23 17:36:27   from invoking the state act as a force majeure event.

24 17:36:30   That is what is needed.

25 17:36:33       The case law on the question of whether the state


                                     232
 1 17:36:36   enterprise is allowed to invoke state act as an event of

 2 17:36:41   force majeure is telling.  Due to the time constraints

 3 17:36:43   I will clearly not stop at each of these cases.  The

 4 17:36:47   analysis was set out in detail in section C1 of the

 5 17:36:51   GPE's rejoinder.

 6 17:36:52       I would only like to note that this table summarises

 9 17:37:05   particularly raised by the claimant.

10 17:37:09       As we can see, the tribunals do not recognise any

11 17:37:12   presumption that an entity where the state has a share

12 17:37:14   is automatically barred from invoking the state's action

13 17:37:18   as force majeure.  No.

14 17:37:20       Rather, they would meticulously engage in

16 17:37:27   depart from the presumption of separate legal

17 17:37:30   personality and declare that the state's act constitute

18 17:37:33   an abuse and, as such, cannot excuse the state's

19 17:37:37   enterprise failure to perform.

20 17:37:40       The case law indicates that neither the level of

21 17:37:45   state's stake or other indication of state's control

22 17:37:49   over the enterprise, nor the state involvement in the

23 17:37:53   conclusion and performance of a particular contract, nor

24 17:37:56   the scope of application of the state act, whether it

25 17:38:00   applied to other entities, but for the responding party

 1 17:38:03   in that dispute, were decisive.  None of these three

23 17:58:58       So first of all, article 102, together with

24 17:59:02   article 101, is actually the pillar of European

25 17:59:07   competition law.  They represent European competition


                                     248
 1 17:59:08   law.

 2 17:59:10       By contrast, REMIT regulation is based on a

 3 17:59:13   different provision of the TFEU, namely article 194,

 4 17:59:18   which is part of TFEU's energy chapter.

 5 17:59:23       So REMIT in essence is concerned with the provision

 6 17:59:26   of harmonised rules for wholesale energy market

 7 17:59:29   transparency and integrity.  It is not concerned with

 8 17:59:33   setting out the competition law rules.

 9 17:59:36       Now, we also have quite distinct regimes in terms of

10 17:59:41   violations and how they established.

11 17:59:43       Article 102 is concerned with abuse of dominance on

12 17:59:47   a relevant product and geographic market.

13 17:59:50       Article 1(2) of the REMIT expressly indicates that

14 17:59:54   its provisions are without prejudice to the application

15 17:59:57   of European competition law, so a breach of REMIT may be

16 18:00:01   completely legitimate under article 102 and vice versa.

17 18:00:05       And the core distinction in relation to the

18 18:00:08   jurisdictional objection by the respondent is in fact

19 18:00:11   the approach to arbitrability of those two instruments.

20 18:00:14       Whereas it is expressly recognised that national

21 18:00:18   courts are entrusted to apply competition law rules as

22 18:00:22   courts of first instance, REMIT has a distinct regime

23 18:00:26   where the function of the court of first instance is

24 18:00:30   entrusted to nationally regulate through authorities.

25 18:00:33       The tribunal can see the relevant provisions on the


                                     249
 1 18:00:35   slides and in fact it is expressly stated in

 2 18:00:39   article 13.1 that the national regulatory authorities

 3 18:00:43   are the bodies which shall ensure that the prohibitions

 4 18:00:46   set out in the REMIT are applied.

 5 18:00:53       For national regulatory authorities to be able to

 6 18:00:55   exercise its function, national states take an

 7 18:00:59   obligation to provide them with investigatory and

 8 18:01:02   enforcement powers.  This is what REMIT expressly says,

 9 18:01:06   it's not even a matter of interpretation.

 3 18:07:28   that will apply both to article 102 and to REMIT.

 4 18:07:32       So European Union's charter provides that everyone

 5 18:07:36   who has been charged shall be presumed innocent until

 6 18:07:39   proved guilty according to law.  And the case law of the

 7 18:07:42   court of justice as well as the wording of the REMIT

 8 18:07:45   support the proposition that this provision is

 9 18:07:48   applicable to any allegations of violations of

10 18:07:52   competition law or REMIT.

11 18:07:54       Now in the present case and based on the case law,

12 18:07:56   PPD bears burden to override presumption of innocence,

13 18:08:00   to prove breach and its causal link with GPE's conduct

14 18:08:05   beyond any doubt and to adduce specific and tangible

15 18:08:10   evidence.  Respectfully, this has not been done.

16 18:08:13       I will start by addressing a violation under

17 18:08:15   article 102.

18 18:08:18       On the next slide the tribunal will see three layers

19 18:08:21   which have to be passed before use of dominance under

20 18:08:25   article 102 can be established, and the burden of

21 18:08:28   proving those three elements lies on the claimant.

22 18:08:32       So, first, the claimant needs to define the relevant

23 18:08:35   market.  Second, it needs to establish respondent's

24 18:08:39   dominance on the relevant market.  And finally, it needs

25 18:08:43   to prove abuse, because being dominant itself is not

 1 18:08:46   a violation.

25 19:01:33   claimant refused to do it, if the impossibility -- the


                                     289
 1 19:01:38   non-performance is on the claimant's side, because

 2 19:01:41   respondent actually gave claimant a mechanism how to

 3 19:01:45   settle its debts sanctions free.

 4 19:01:48       For those reasons, articles 11 and 10 of the

 5 19:01:52   relevant EU sanctions regulations are plainly

 6 19:01:55   inapplicable in the present case.  They do not apply on

 7 19:01:58   any occasion to the claim with respect to the two months

 8 19:02:03   of unpaid gas supplies.

 9 19:02:05       Finally, claimant cannot rely on them to avoid those

10 19:02:09   payments because it was provided with a concrete

11 19:02:11   mechanism where those payments would be settled directly

12 19:02:14   to respondent's account, and without the use of the

13 19:02:17   payment mechanism.

14 19:02:19       This ends my submission.  Thank you very much.
